Text:"Scaling Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning for Brain Encoding Josh Ying1,2, Guanglong Sun3, Nolan Ho2, Kieran Liu2, Rui Yang2,4, Andrew Chen2, Nikolaus Kriegeskorte1, Pony Ma2, Hossein Adeli1 1Columbia University, 2Mind Lab, 3Tsinghua University, 4HKUST(GZ) Brain encoding models aim to predict neural responses to stimuli. The most widely used approach fits readouts from frozen pretrained backbones. Using neural data to train large models from scratch or fully fine-tune them after task-pretraining has proven difficult because neural data is costly and limited. We show that parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) enables us to effectively adapt very large pretrained backbones for brain encoding with small number of trainable parameters. To make it computationally practical, we exploit the backbones causal structure to predict multiple timepoints in single pass instead of using sliding windows, significantly reducing feature-extraction cost. This enables efficient fine-tuning of multimodal backbones with up to 104B parameters, the largest model adapted for brain encoding to our knowledge. Trained with low-rank adapter (LoRA) and lightweight readout, our approach consistently improves brain encoding performance across nine diverse multimodal backbones. On the same Qwen3-Omni backbone as the prior state-of-the-art (SoTA), our method BrainLoRA achieves the best published performance on the Algonauts 2025 Challenge, using only about 5% as many trainable parameters. We also show the adapted multimodal backbones remain interpretable: modality attribution recovers the canonical sensory topography that prior work often obtains by construction from separate unimodal encoders, and neural adaptation increases the contribution of audio inputs and cross-modal mixing within the backbone. To scale out the number of adapted models, we develop multi-LoRA kernel that accelerates joint training of multiple LoRAs on shared backbone. This enables an ensemble of 200+ adapted models that ranks f"

Text:"Self-Evolving Coding Agents via Harness-Policy Optimization Zhepei Wei1,2, Wenlin Yao1, Baolin Peng1, Danqing Wang3, Qianhui Wu1 Hao Cheng1, Yu Meng2, Jianfeng Gao1 1Microsoft Research 3Carnegie Mellon University 2University of Virginia https://github.com/MSR-Orchard/Self-HPO https://huggingface.co/self-hpo Abstract Large language model (LLM) agents have shown promising potential in coding tasks, and their performance is jointly determined by the policy model and the harness that mediates their interaction with the environment. Indeed, harness and policy are mutually reinforcing and evolving only one component is suboptimala harness tuned for one checkpoint may not target the failure modes of an updated policy, while policy learning remains limited to the trajectories elicited by fixed harness. In light of this, we propose Self-HPO, recursive self-improving framework for coding agents that alternates harness and policy optimization, using the evolving policy as its own harness proposer. Specifically, in each round, the current policy first analyzes its own failures and proposes new harness candidates (H-step). The policy is then updated through reinforcement learning (RL) under the selected harness (P-step), and hard tasks identified during training form the next harness search set, keeping harness optimization targeted to the policys evolving failure modes. Across three coding benchmarks, Self-HPO consistently outperforms harness-only and policy-only baselines, improving Qwen3.5-35B-A3Bs average resolve rate from 57.8% to 64.8%, with gains generalizing to out-of-distribution settings. Further analysis shows that both optimized components retain gains even when paired with their base counterparts, while their joint gain exceeds the sum of their individual gains. Self-HPO also improves reliability (pass@1) and expands the capability frontier (pass@k) over the base agent. Additionally, we show that with stronger external model (e.g., Opus 4.8) as the harness proposer,"

Constructing training experience for these capabilities requires coherent requests, service records, tool interfaces, and grading checks. We present CoworkEnv, capability-guided framework for synthesizing executable training environments for AI coworkers. Workflow requirements guide task construction, and programmatic validation with feedback repair checks generated specifications and grading behavior. Teacher executions yield curated demonstrations for supervised fine-tuning (SFT), while student interactions provide rollouts and rewards for reinforcement learning (RL). Using these resources, we train compact PocketWorker models. For Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, SFT raises the mean score from 46.6 to 64.1 on Claw-Eval, including 31.9-point gain on Multi-turn tasks. An SFT-only extension to ClawMark demonstrates applicability across coworker benchmarks. We further compare RL training dynamics across configurations that vary reward processing and credit assignment. Improving training rewards provide preliminary evidence that the constructed environments support policy optimization. 1. Text:"2026-10-06 MemPilot: Orchestrating On-Demand Multimodal Memory Curation for LLM Agents Haozhen Zhang1, Haodong Yue2, Quanyu Long1, Jianzhu Bao1, Qingyuan Liu1, Tao Feng3, Bohan Liu1, Weida Liang1 and Wenya Wang1 1Nanyang Technological University, 2Tsinghua University, 3University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign (cid:130) Project Website MemPilot MemPilot Collection Memory has become integral to the LLM agent ecosystem, supporting information retention and reuse across interactions. However, most existing agent memory systems construct memory in query-agnostic manner, which can incur unnecessary preprocessing cost and discard details that later prove essential. Recent studies have begun shifting memory processing toward runtime adaptation, but typically specialize in particular operations or fixed processing schemes, leaving flexible control over performance, cost, and latency largely underexplored. To address this challenge, we present MemPilot, flexible framework that orchestrates on-demand memory curation under different performancecostlatency preferences. Specifically, we optimize multi-step LLM policy via reinforcement learning to iteratively choose between retrieving from query-agnostic memory and delegating query-specific curation of raw multimodal history to heterogeneous LLMs and VLMs. The policy jointly controls evidence amount, curation instructions, model selection, and visual access, enabling fine-grained allocation of runtime computation. To optimize this policy under competing objectives, we adapt objective-wise advantage decoupling by separately estimating each objectives advantage before aggregation. Moreover, we introduce prefix-based marginal utility estimation for fine-grained credit assignment across multi-step rollouts. Experiments on five multimodal agent-memory benchmarks demonstrate favorable performancecostlatency trade-offs across optimization preferences, with preference sweeps yielding broader frontiers than existing trade-off-aware baseline"

Existing VLM-guided procedural methods remain largely constrained by predefined factories and APIs, limiting their ability to create novel asset types. Text:"Fr echet Forcing: Training Long-Horizon Streaming World Models from Short Clips Yian Zhao, Yu Yan, Hongcan Guo, Ruochong Zheng, Jian Zhang, Jie Chen Abstract Streaming video world models generate the future chunk by chunk, conditioned on their own past and stream of actions. When trained on short clips, they drift once rollout exceeds the training horizon, as prediction errors re-enter the context and compound. common remedy is to train the model on its own rollouts via distribution matching distillation (DMD). For action-conditioned world models, however, the teacher becomes bottleneck: an external video model is agnostic to the actions, whereas the models own pretrained network drifts in the same manner as the student. We observe that long rollout need not match teacher, provided it remains realistic continuation of its own scene that follows the commanded actions. Realism and scene fidelity can be measured directly against real-data statistics, which remain valid at any horizon, while action following is preserved by the models conditional generation loss. Building on this insight, we propose Fréchet Forcing, which fine-tunes world model together with its deployed multi-step sampler under three complementary constraints. Generative anchoring applies the flow-matching loss after self-generated history ending on real data, preserving action-conditioned generation. Context fidelity, Fréchet distance (FD) on latent statistics of the free rollout centred on each clips own ground truth, keeps every rollout faithful to its scene. Reality matching, an FD to real frames in frozen perceptual space, keeps the generated frames realistic. Because all three targets are derived from short real clips without comparing rollout against long recorded future, training requires no teacher, no online critic and no long videos. Fine-tuning MiniWorld-1B, pretrained on 253-frame clips, for at most 36 GPU-hours on 381-frame clips yields rollouts that remain stable beyond 4,000 frames and "

Text:"LIBERO-Industry: Benchmarking Robotic Manipulation under Industrial Process Constraints Ming Shin Cindy Sung1, Duan Leijiahao1, Haoxiang Wang1, Boning Tan1, Liangna Fu1, Chao Tan1, Yafu Li2, Yulong Chen3,4, Chaoqun Wan1, Zhen Huang1, Shuxian Liang1, Xian-Sheng Hua1 1Institute of AI for Engineering, Tongji University 2The Chinese University of Hong Kong 3University of Aberdeen 4University of Cambridge https://IAIE-PhysicalAI.github.io/LIBERO-Industry Abstract Industrial manipulation in flexible manufacturing, e.g., high-mix, low-volume (HMLV) electronics assembly, requires robot policies to adapt to changing products and production conditions. Such settings impose industrial process constraints, including product variants, moving material flows, finite execution windows, and tight geometric tolerances. However, existing evaluation frameworks do not systematically capture these constraints when assessing generalist robot policies. To address this gap, we introduce LIBERO-Industry, novel benchmark built on the widely used robot learning benchmark LIBERO, that translates the aforementioned industrial constraints into controllable task conditions and measurable success criteria. Specifically, LIBERO-Industry comprises three classic task scenarios: Static-toDynamic (S2D) loading, Dynamic-to-Static (D2S) unloading, and Dynamic-to-Dynamic (D2D) transfer. The suites contain 216 tasks in total that systematically vary object geometry, conveyor speed, interaction-window duration, and geometric acceptance region size. Extensive experiments across five generalist robot policies (including vision-language-action models and world-action models) show that performance progressively degrades as industrial constraints are introduced and combined. Text:"ECHO: Efficient Causal Self-Recursion Beyond Auxiliary Multi-Token Prediction Yuxuan Zhou1, Junyuan Shang1, Tianyu Wang2, Chunrui Zheng1, Zichang Liu1, Wenbin Chang1, Shuohuan Wang1, Dianhai Yu1 2Independent Researcher 1ERNIE Team, Baidu https://github.com/ernie-research/ECHO Abstract Existing Multi-token prediction (MTP) methods are auxiliary by design: the multihorizon loss mostly trains modules attached to the backbone, and each added horizon costs another module and another pass. We propose Efficient Causal Self-Recursion (ECHO), which removes both costs. The backbones own final layer predicts every horizon, so the supervision lands on the parameters that generate the next token, and Dynamic Depth Sampling (DDS) keeps deeper horizons affordable by drawing the training depth with probabilities that decay along depth. Future prediction thus becomes part of the autoregressive objective rather than task attached to it, and the gains follow on both sides of it. In pretraining, added depth converts into downstream accuracy where auxiliary modules saturate, concentrating on reasoning and code at half the MTP training overhead of training every depth. At inference, the pretrained model is its own drafter: it drafts beyond the depth it was trained for, and keeps its draft-target alignment on the open-ended, high-entropy generation where post-hoc drafters lose theirs. The advantage survives doubled token budget, repeated pretraining run, and supervised finetuning. Text:"A Conditional No-Hair Theorem for Transformers: How Language Models Forget Their Prompt October 2026 Abstract black hole retains only few conserved charges of the matter it formed from. We ask how much an autoregressive transformer retains of its prompt after generating tokens. Consider two different prompts followed by the same continuation text, so that any difference in their hidden states comes from the prompt alone, and we find that the distance between their hidden states decays approximately as power law tα, where α = 1 is the rate at which fixed prompts share of uniform attention shrinks and smaller values mean longer memory. Beyond the first layers, trained models forget fast (α 0.550.7), while untrained models with the same architecture forget slowly (α 0.20.5, decreasing with depth). We prove that this offset is O(cid:0)P (log t)L1/t(cid:1) for prompt of length and depth L. The bound holds for finite-depth pre-LN softmax transformers with full causal attention and bounded attention logits (e.g. rotary position encodings), under one additional condition: the pre-norm activations stay away from zero. The mechanism is geometric: the prompts direct influence is diluted as the context grows, and relay continuation tokens passing on what earlier tokens read from the prompt can only add logarithmic factors. This is what we call the conditional no-hair theorem: given the text generated so far, the prompts influence on the next token vanishes as grows, so any property of the prompt that survives, such as its language, must be carried by the text itself. An exactly solvable linear relay model shows that the bound cannot be improved and reproduces the rise of the local exponent towards 1 seen in untrained networks. Cancelling the dilution with logit bias on the prompt stops the decay early in training and makes the offset grow after training, so dilution is necessary for the forgetting we measure; untrained networks and the solvable model show that it is also suffic"

Text:"Factorization-Guided Training and Inference for Diffusion-Drafted Speculative Decoding Jiakun Fan1, 3, Min Xiao2, 3, Qiang Wang3, Neiwen Ling3, Zhichao Liu3, Zhihao Jia2, Dimitrios S. Nikolopoulos1, Zijun Wei3 1Virginia Tech, 2Carnegie Mellon University, 3ByteDance Equal contribution, Corresponding author Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference through parallel verification of tokens proposed by drafter. Diffusion drafters further parallelize proposal generation, but greater proposal capacity or higher mean accepted tokens (MAT) need not yield higher throughput. Because verification accepts only consecutive prefix, early rejections can render later correct predictions ineffective, limiting MAT gains, while even higher MAT may not improve throughput if per-round latency increases. We introduce DScout, which uses two complementary factorizations to guide diffusion drafter training and inference for higher throughput. For training, DScout factorizes position-wise loss into inclusion, weighting, and prediction loss. This guides the design and combination of mixedhorizon training, intra-block unmasking, and acceptance-weighted loss to align supervision with prefix acceptance and improve MAT. For inference, DScout factorizes throughput as the ratio of MAT to per-round latency, guiding target-conditioning depth, drafter depth, and inference block size selection to identify high-throughput configuration for each workload. Across six benchmarks, DScout achieves 3.84 and 4.14 mean speedups over autoregressive decoding on Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B, respectively, improving mean speedup over the state-of-the-art baseline by 9.1% and 7.1%. Across models and concurrency settings, our inference configuration policy provides up to 9.75% additional mean speedup over DScouts fixed configuration. Text:"HandsOn 2026-10-13 HandsOn: Large-Scale Simulation Benchmark for Generalizable Dexterous Manipulation Tianwei Luo1,3,,, Yiming He1,,, Hongjin Wu1,, Ting Pan2,, Debin Ren3,, Haorui Guo3, Junlin Yang1, Yuxin Zuo1, Zhehao Kou1, Kaisen Yang1, Zhuoqun Lai3, Yi Ding3 Jiale Yu1, Yuchen Zhang4, Zijie Qiu1, Haozhan Li1,3, Ning Ding1,3, 1Tsinghua University 3NatureWill # luotw23@mails.tsinghua.edu.cn, dingning@tsinghua.edu.cn Code: https://github.com/TsinghuaC3I/HandsOn 2Beijing University of Posts and Telecommunications 4Peking University Equal contribution. Text:"Sparse Feature Policy Unlearning Mitigates State Hallucination in Vision-Language-Action Models Jiho Lee1, Jeongeun Park2, Heayoun Choi1, Taekyung Kim3,, and Eunwoo Kim1, Abstract Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have shown strong generalization in robotic manipulation by leveraging rich representations from pretrained vision-language models. However, their deployment in real-world environments remains limited by recurring unreliable behaviors. In this work, we study state hallucination, recurring failure pattern in which VLA continues acting as if an unrealized robot-object state had been achieved. Our analyses find that state hallucination coincides with weakened attention to task-relevant visual regions, and mechanistic interpretation via sparse autoencoders reveals that hallucination-associated sparse features are activated when these failures occur. Based on this analysis, we propose SOUL (Sparse feature pOlicy UnLearning), which selectively unlearns policy knowledge associated with state hallucination behaviors, where sparse features identified from hallucination failures and successful behaviors serve as explicit forgetting and retention targets, respectively. Experiments across VLA architectures in simulated and real-world environments show that our method substantially reduces hallucinated failures and improves task success without substantially compromising the existing manipulation capabilities. These results suggest that interpretable feature analysis provides practical basis for selectively modifying undesirable knowledge in robot policies. I. Text:"Wenqian Ye1,2,*, Ziwei Guan1, Zi Yang1, Shivani Modi1, Cici Cong Wu1, Yunlong He1, Yuxin Chen1, Zhengyu Zhang1, Liang Luo1, Venkatesh Ranganathan1, Jianhui Sun1, Yantao Yao1, Wenlin Chen1, Buyun Zhang1,, Ellie Dingqiao Wen1,, Henry Kautz2,, Aidong Zhang2, 1Meta AI, 2University of Virginia Work done at Meta Recent advances in self-improving LLM agents have demonstrated the potential of AI systems that drive their own intelligence. However, many domain-specific applications rely on specialized foundation models that possess strong domain expertise but remain dependent on human-designed improvements. Extending recursive self-improvement to these models presents key challenge: as specialist improves, the strategies used to advance it must also adapt. To address this challenge, we propose Heterogeneous Agentic CoEvolution (HACE), framework that jointly evolves specialized foundation models and LLM agents through reciprocal learning. HACE improves the specialist through supervised learning while training the agent with on-policy reinforced self-training, guided by verified performance gains on the current specialist. The agents exploration, in turn, informs subsequent specialist training, enabling both models to adapt to each others progress. To sustain this reciprocal learning, HACE further employs evolutionary search to optimize their interaction harness, adapting how the models exchange information and select training experiences as their capabilities evolve. We evaluate HACE across three challenging domains: recommendation systems, protein function prediction in biology, and crystal stability prediction in material discovery. We show that HACE outperforms the evaluated self-improving methods on the primary task metrics across all three domains. These results shed light on heterogeneous coevolution as universal approach to improving specialized foundation models through reciprocal interaction with learning agent. Text:"Vision-Language-Delta-Action: Learning Compact Deltas as Actionable Transition Interfaces for Robotic Manipulation Lingsen Zhang1,2, Wei Li1,2, Jie He1,2 Henghua Zhang2 Kai Mo2 Shiyong Li2 Yanpeng Wang2 Rui Shao1,3, Equal Contribution Corresponding Author 1Harbin Institute of Technology, Shenzhen 2Baidu, Inc. 3Shenzhen Loop Area Institute Robotic manipulation is not merely observation-to-action generation, but the realization of task-specified state changes in the physical world. Existing vision-language-action (VLA) methods directly learn observation-toaction mappings, imposing substantial learning burden on policies due to the large gap between semantic understanding and action execution. Although world-action models (WAMs) improve control through future prediction, they optimize the full future rather than task-relevant changes, wasting capacity on static appearance or action-irrelevant abstractions. To address this issue, we propose Vision-Language-Delta-Action (VLDA), new paradigm that reformulates full future prediction as compact actionable transition modeling. Our core insight is to introduce compact Delta as an intermediate decision variable that captures latent world-state changes, serving as unified representational interface between vision-language understanding and action generation. To learn such Delta representation, VLDA introduces unified objective composed of the Local Transition Filter (LT-Filter) and Global Transition Anchor (GT-Anchor). LT-Filter captures taskrelevant manipulation-centric changes, preventing Delta from being biased toward static patch reconstruction. GT-Anchor provides coarse future-level regularization, ensuring that Delta can explain the overall trend of future changes. Building on these, Delta-Driven Action Refinement (DDAR) further grounds Delta in the action manifold and injects it as structured seed to guide action denoising. We implement VLDA within LoongForge, an open-source training framework for large-scale foundation "

We extend this paradigm to recommendation for decision-making, focusing on decisions where the set of possible choices is fixed but the reasons for choosing them are not. In high-stakes decisions, e.g., which company to acquire or which customer to target, comparing diverse, well-reasoned options can be more useful than single ranked list. We formalize openended recommendation as building diverse, high-quality set of recommendations, each an item together with hypothesis, natural-language reason why the item fits the needs of decision maker. We propose QD-Rec, qualitydiversity method that selects items with contextual bandit and generates hypotheses with foundation model from earlier ones kept in an archive as stepping stones. new recommendation enters the archive if it is novel or better than its nearest neighbor. Text:"Beyond Maliciousness: Static and Runtime Attack-Surface Profiling of Agent Skills Ratnesh Pandeya, Japneet Singha, Harsh Akshita, Sonali Tyagib, Cassie Crossleyc aMetano Labs bPurdue University cVulNow AbstractAgent skills are Markdown documents that toolusing agent reads as instructions and then acts on with the privileges of the session that loaded them. Installing one grants authority (the standing to direct the agent, not new privilege), and the size of that grant is what we measure. We present two-phase attack-surface profiling methodology that combines whole-bundle static analysis and dependency provenance checking with instrumented sandbox execution and separate model-based assessment of the resulting traces. We apply the methodology to 6,309 listings from two marketplaces, selecting listings for execution without conditioning that decision on prior threat or risk signal. Half the corpus carries evidence of shell-execution capability; the corresponding shares are 35% for filesystem read, 31% each for filesystem write and network egress, and 13% for credential access; seven listings in ten carry at least one elevated capability. That is the surface user accepts at install time, and almost none of it is visible then. permission model exists but is close to inert. Authors may list the tools skill pre-approves in its own frontmatter, which removes confirmation prompts rather than restricting what the skill can reach; of the 6,061 listings whose frontmatter parses, 770 declare anything at all, the vocabulary cannot express credential or environment access, and nothing checks what it does express against the bundle. Declaring is also not declaring accurately: 59% of declaring listings understate what their bundle can do. ClawHub declares roughly twice as often as skillsmp, yet underdeclares on 88% of its declarations against 31%. Triage at the marketplace boundary therefore cannot rest on what listing declares. It must rest on what the bundle can do. These results "

People ask conversational AI models about their legal rights, obligations, procedures, and personal situations that may have legal consequences. To study this behavior, we first need to distinguish requests for legal guidance from other legally themed discussions and unrelated conversations. We contribute multi-axis taxonomy of legal queries capturing whether legal guidance is sought, how request is formulated, and which legal domains it concerns; apply it to 2,122 naturally occurring WildChat-1M conversations; and create human-reviewed gold evaluation set of 200 conversations. All annotations, model checkpoints, and evaluation code are publicly available. In this enriched sample, 71.1% of conversations containing legal content seek personalized or practical guidance. Rights-and-options advice is the most common primary request type among legal-content conversations, and 25.3% of guidance-positive conversations span multiple legal topics. The gold evaluation set supports two-stage comparison of ModernBERT-base and ModernBERT-large against TFIDF cascade. Our framework supports large-scale studies of legal guidance in conversational AI, and the public release of the annotated data facilitates reproducible research and future model development. During interactions with robot, its onboard state information may be unavailable due to privacy, ownership, or communication constraints. Text:"Yuxuan Liu Department of Mathematics, University of Bergen, Bergen, Norway yuxuan.liu@uib.no October 2026 Abstract Augmenting softmax attention with forget gates typically relies on summing decay contributions along the keyquery path. Because such accumulation is monotonic, these mechanisms cannot selectively erase intermediate context while preserving older, structurally relevant tokens. We propose First Passage Attention (FPA), which shifts from path summation to path extrema by lowering keys logit proportionally to the deepest dip of learned scalar field below that keys level over the intervening span. FPA encompasses the Forgetting Transformer (FoX) as special case, and when the field and levels follow shared learned walk, the alive keys with unpenalised logits naturally form stack. We show that constant-dimension FPA head realises any subset of keys left alive by subsequent dips, whereas softmax heads with positional biases or token-level forget gates require head dimension linear in the number of keys. In synthetic experiments, one-layer FPA heads converge to these target sets while comparable softmax and FoX baselines fail on every seed. Scaling to 110M-parameter language model, FPA matches FoX in perplexity and uniquely achieves perfect passkey retrieval at 8 the training length, even with its keyvalue cache compressed to about 25%. Transformers power most modern large language models, with softmax attention serving as their central operation (Vaswani et al., 2017; Jiang et al., 2023). Standard attention scores each earlier token by matching its key against the current query, typically incorporating relative positional encodings such as rotary embeddings or distance penalties (Press et al., 2022; Su et al., 2024). Intervening tokens between key and query do not directly enter this score. In many sequences, however, subsequent event renders earlier information obsolete. backspace deletes the last typed character, closing bracket terminates an enclosed scope, "

Abstract Parameter-efficient fine-tuning with low-rank adapters enables efficient adaptation of very large multimodal backbones for brain encoding, improving performance and interpretability while reducing computational cost.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Brain encoding models aim to predict neural responses to stimuli. The most widely used approach fits readouts from frozen pretrained backbones. Using neural data to train large models from scratch or fully fine-tune them after task-pretraining has proven difficult because neural data is costly and limited. We show that parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) enables us to effectively adapt very large pretrained backbones for brain encoding with a small number of trainable parameters. To make it computationally practical, we exploit the backbone's causal structure to predict multiple timepoints in a single pass instead of using sliding windows, significantly reducing feature-extraction cost. This enables efficient fine-tuning of multimodal backbones with up to 104B parameters, the largest model adapted for brain encoding to our knowledge. Trained with a low-rank adapter (LoRA) and a lightweight readout, our approach consistently improves brain encoding performance across nine diverse multimodal backbones. On the same Qwen3-Omni backbone as the prior state-of-the-art (SoTA), our method BrainLoRA achieves the best published performance on the Algonauts 2025 Challenge. We also show the adapted multimodal backbones remain interpretable: modality attribution recovers the canonical sensory topography that prior work often obtains by construction from separate unimodal encoders, and neural adaptation increases the contribution of audio inputs and cross-modal mixing within the backbone. To scale out the number of adapted models, we develop a multi-LoRA kernel that accelerates joint training of multiple LoRAs on a shared backbone. This enables an ensemble of 200+ adapted models that ranks first on the public Algonauts post-challenge leaderboard at the time of writing. Together, our results highlight PEFT as an efficient and powerful paradigm for brain encoding.

Abstract OR-License evaluates whether LLM agents can solve real-world operations research problems from original client materials, finding that while some achieve high objective quality, none match expert-level feasibility and deployment-adjusted quality.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Can large language model (LLM) agents deliver expert-level operations research (OR) solutions directly from original client materials, without prior expert reformulation? Existing OR benchmarks typically begin with self-contained descriptions or predefined optimization tasks, underrepresenting the translation of original business materials into professionally validated solutions. We introduce OR-License, an end-to-end benchmark of 31 instances across 10 industrial scenarios from paid OR engagements totaling USD 3.68 million. It preserves original requirements, input data, deliverable formats, and information boundaries after de-identification. To study information acquisition, we introduce ORSim, a controlled client simulator that discloses business facts without supplying OR expertise. Hidden evaluation checks feasibility and compares objective quality against original expert solutions accepted by clients before benchmark construction. Deployment-Adjusted Quality (DAQ) averages expert-relative quality across instances, assigning zero to infeasible deliveries. Across 11 frontier LLMs using coding-agent harnesses, several exceed the expert reference in conditional objective quality, but none matches its DAQ; the strongest primary Claude Code configuration achieves 88.39% strict feasibility and a DAQ of 0.87 against the expert reference of 1. Client information acquisition extends beyond clarification into solution development and validation, with some later stages showing higher confirmed issue-resolution proportions. Trajectory analysis identifies formulation and implementation failures, while task-equalized research-chain completion rates range from 24% to 45% across models on evaluable tasks. These findings motivate sustained information-gap resolution, faithful problem formulation, and evidence-linked self-improvement for reliable long-horizon agents.

Abstract EgoXperience introduces a unified framework for joint reconstruction of camera motion, scene geometry, hands, and objects from egocentric video using automatically annotated large-scale data and a single-pass model.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Reconstructing human activity from egocentric video requires more than recovering individual 3D components: the observer's motion, surrounding geometry, and hand-object interactions must fit together in a consistent world. Achieving this coherence requires both joint reconstruction and aligned supervision, yet existing methods and datasets offer limited support for either. Abstract MemT-1.0 introduces a memory-native architecture that embeds continuous memory into foundation model computation, improving long-context efficiency and agentic task performance.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Memory-native evolution requires making memory an intrinsic capability of foundation models, enabling them to continuously accumulate and utilize information. To this end, we introduce MemT-1.0, a new architecture of memory foundation models that internalize memory as part of model computation. Building on Metis, MemT-1.0 employs Gated DeltaNet-2 with a stable token-level memory update mechanism within each turn. We further introduce Mixture-of-Storage to scale native memory capacity. Following a deliberate backbone preparation, we develop a two-stage training pipeline consisting of continued pre-training and post-training, supported by 34B tokens of training data and efficient training and inference infrastructure. We release a model family comprising MemT-1.0-Nano and MemT-1.0-Flash, and conduct a comprehensive evaluation of memory and general capabilities. The results show that they outperform the parametric memory baselines on average and remain competitive with retrieval-augmented methods, while largely preserving general capabilities. At a history length of 128K tokens, MemT-1.0-Nano achieves approximately 1.86times end-to-end inference speedup and a 66times reduction in persistent storage relative to full context KV cache. By internalizing retrieved procedural experience, MemT-1.0 improves task success on agentic tasks, providing a practical step toward memory-native evolution.

Abstract Robots share execution experience across fleets to improve policies via failure-guided retrieval and asynchronous training, achieving high success without extra expert corrections.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Experience generated during deployment offers an opportunity for robots to improve policy reliability through learning from their own executions. However, learning through human corrections requires teleoperators to monitor execution and intervene when policies fail, a demand that is difficult to meet as robot fleets grow. We introduce collective experience learning (CEL), a learning paradigm in which deployed robots share their execution experience to improve a common policy. We realize CEL through failure-guided experience retrieval fine-tuning (FERT), which uses failure contexts from robot executions to retrieve relevant trajectory segments from the fleet's shared successful experience and uses their actions as supervision for policy updates, without collecting additional expert demonstrations or corrective actions. A fault-tolerant asynchronous training system (FATS) coordinates experience collection, policy optimization and policy distribution despite local interruptions within the robot fleet. Across five real-world manipulation tasks, the resulting policies achieve success rates of 93.3-99.2%, evaluated over 120 trials per task on robots excluded from training data collection. With collection time per robot held fixed, larger fleets provide more experience and FERT generally achieves higher success rates across the evaluated sizes. On multi-stage phone packaging, learning with 96 robots increases success from 79.2% to 98.3%. These results support collective experience as a practical basis for scalable robot learning and a path towards improving policy reliability during deployment without collecting additional expert corrective actions.

Abstract OpenVLHarness improves multimodal reasoning by integrating persistent memory, heterogeneous tool interfaces, and iterative evidence composition across 22 benchmarks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made rapid progress on visual reasoning, yet many tasks require evidence that is difficult to obtain through internal reasoning alone: our controlled study on 22 benchmarks shows that simply increasing the inference budget of the base model yields only modest gains. Such tasks demand grounded perception, external knowledge, and numerical measurements obtained through heterogeneous tools, whose outputs must be made interpretable to the model and retained across reasoning steps. We introduce OpenVLHarness, a multimodal reasoning harness organized around a capability layer and persistent multimodal memory. The capability layer combines task-level tool interfaces with evidence rendering, providing consistent access to heterogeneous backends and converting their outputs into model-readable observations. Persistent multimodal memory retains the corresponding images, structured data, and web references as addressable artifacts throughout each query. A coding interface executes generated programs over stored data, enabling computations that combine results from multiple tools. Together, these mechanisms support an iterative process of acquiring, inspecting, and composing multimodal evidence. Evaluated on 22 benchmarks with eight open and proprietary backbones, OpenVLHarness improves average scores by 9.7-12.6 points over matched no-tool baselines, including +11.2 with Qwen3-VL-8B, +9.7 with Kimi K3, and +9.9 with GPT-6 Astra. With GPT-6 Luna at medium reasoning effort, OpenVLHarness outperforms the base model, Codex, and Visual Sketchpad at maximum effort by 7.7-8.5 points while incurring at most 37% of their model-token cost.

Abstract ScaleNav learns goal-directed visual dynamics from internet egocentric videos to generate robot actions for language-conditioned local navigation, achieving high success rates in simulation and real-world deployment.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small We study language-conditioned local navigation, where a robot must navigate to a text-specified target visible in its initial observation and stop at the described location. End-to-end navigation policies require diverse training data to generalize across scenes, yet real-robot data is costly to collect and simulated data leaves a sim-to-real gap. Internet egocentric videos offer a scalable source of real-world navigation experience. Our key insight is to learn goal-directed visual dynamics from Internet egocentric videos, and use these dynamics to support robot action generation. To this end, we propose ScaleNav, a unified world-action framework with three-stage training. ScaleNav achieves success rates of 86.5% on a single-view adaptation of Short-Horizon OVON and 94.9% on our HabitatGS-LN benchmark, outperforming the evaluated baselines on both benchmarks. We further deploy and evaluate ScaleNav on a real quadruped robot, with results supporting the model's ability to generalize to real-world navigation. Code and videos are available at https://zju3dv.github.io/ScaleNav/

Abstract SpecMRL improves compressed embeddings by reorganizing features via a learned spectral basis that prioritizes informative directions, outperforming standard MRL at reduced dimensions.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Matryoshka Representation Learning (MRL) is designed to support flexible representation sizes by jointly optimizing nested prefixes. While effective, this design lacks an explicit constraint to ensure that the leading portion provides a suitable compact encoding. To address this, we propose SpecMRL, which reorganizes the feature space to enable more effective prefix readout. These results suggest that the learned spectral basis better prioritizes informative directions, enabling more effective representation compression. Notably, SpecMRL extends the performance frontier of MRL, surpassing full-dimensional MRL with only half the embedding dimensions.

Abstract Self-HPO is a recursive framework that alternates harness and policy optimization for coding agents, improving performance by targeting evolving failure modes through reinforcement learning.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large language model (LLM) agents have shown promising potential in coding tasks, and their performance is jointly determined by the policy model and the harness that mediates their interaction with the environment. Indeed, harness and policy are mutually reinforcing and evolving only one component is suboptimal---a harness tuned for one checkpoint may not target the failure modes of an updated policy, while policy learning remains limited to the trajectories elicited by a fixed harness. In light of this, we propose Self-HPO, a recursive self-improving framework for coding agents that alternates harness and policy optimization, using the evolving policy as its own harness proposer. Specifically, in each round, the current policy first analyzes its own failures and proposes new harness candidates (H-step). The policy is then updated through reinforcement learning (RL) under the selected harness (P-step), and hard tasks identified during training form the next harness search set, keeping harness optimization targeted to the policy's evolving failure modes. Across three coding benchmarks, Self-HPO consistently outperforms harness-only and policy-only baselines, improving Qwen3.5-35B-A3B's average resolve rate from 57.8% to 64.8%, with gains generalizing to out-of-distribution settings. Further analysis shows that both optimized components retain gains even when paired with their base counterparts, while their joint gain exceeds the sum of their individual gains. Self-HPO also improves reliability (Pass@1) and expands the capability frontier (Pass@k) over the base agent. Additionally, we show that with a stronger external model (e.g., Opus 4.8) as the harness proposer, the same framework yields a further performance boost.

Abstract MT Forcing improves multi-turn image editing by combining single-turn distribution matching distillation and teacher-forcing guidance to stabilize visual quality and preserve cross-turn consistency.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Multi-turn image editing aims to modify an image according to a sequence of instructions, enabling more effective interactive visual content creation for users. However, existing methods often exhibit noticeable drift after only a few editing turns, manifesting in two aspects: 1) unstable visual quality, with accumulating artifacts and degraded details; and 2) inconsistent image content, with unintended changes to preserved regions and prior edits. To address these challenges, we propose MT Forcing, a novel training paradigm for multi-turn image editing with unified multimodal models (UMMs) based on autoregressive diffusion. Specifically, we propose Single-Turn Distribution Matching Distillation (Single-Turn DMD), which supervises student self-rollouts with a teacher that applies cumulative edits to the source image in a single turn, providing visual-quality guidance without recursive error accumulation. Additionally, we introduce Teacher-Forcing Guidance, which supervises history-dependent transitions on reference trajectories during warmup and as a regularizer during distillation, encouraging cross-turn content consistency beyond what per-turn distribution matching alone can ensure. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach significantly improves the multi-turn image editing capabilities of UMMs, yielding more stable visual quality and stronger cross-turn consistency.

Abstract Interlock synthesizes long-horizon tasks as typed completion graphs to enforce cross-component constraints, then uses agent-based verification and self-training to improve agent performance on complex benchmarks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large language model agents increasingly undertake long-horizon tasks in complex environments characterized by fragmented evidence, partially completed states, and dependencies across resources and artifacts. Existing agents may satisfy individual requirements while failing to maintain these relationships, producing locally correct but incomplete outcomes. These failures arise because task completion depends on relationships among environmental evidence, consequential decisions, and required effects. To address this completion gap, we propose Interlock, a framework for task synthesis that represents each task as a typed completion graph. Grounding edges connect public evidence to decisions, coupling hyperedges impose joint constraints across components, and realization edges connect decisions to all required downstream effects. Interlock compiles this graph into a resettable workspace, a public request, and an evaluation rubric. A source-coverage review checks whether the original requirements survive generation, while a public-input review checks whether the rubric is supported by the materials available to the solver. An agent-as-judge inspects execution artifacts and runs verification scripts, retaining trajectories judged to satisfy all mandatory requirements. To evaluate training utility, we fine-tune two Qwen3.6 backbones on their own accepted trajectories. Across the two backbones, training improves AutomationBench task pass rates by 4.00-4.50 percentage points, JobBench weighted scores by 3.20-6.64 points, and Harvey LAB criterion pass rates by 3.89-4.16 percentage points. These results demonstrate the training utility of the synthesized experience across external task environments.

Abstract Researchers introduce AnyGoal to evaluate whether world models can generalize dynamics to unseen combinations of familiar states and actions for planning.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small The goal of a world model is to capture the dynamics of an environment. This has the great appeal that it allows agents to plan and simulate a course of actions with the help of a world model. For these uses to be reliable, however, the learned dynamics must behave well beyond the specific state-action combinations observed in expert rollouts. Despite this, the relationship between data coverage, dynamics generalization, and downstream planning performance remains underexplored. We study this relationship through the lens of compositional generalization: can a world model recombine familiar state and action factors into valid dynamics? To answer this, we introduce AnyGoal, a benchmark of goal-shifted task variants built from widely used world-model environments, together with an open-source library. Each task is designed to be within reach of a world model that has learned the underlying dynamics: individual state factors and actions remain within their training support, but their combinations are underrepresented in expert trajectories. Across multiple environments and world model families, we find that current models consistently exhibit failures under these recombinations. By examining dynamics and representation failures across model families, interaction regimes, and evaluation metrics, we study where current world models generalize and where challenges remain for goal-shifted prediction and planning. Together, this framework, benchmark, and analysis provide a basis for evaluating progress toward world models that generalize to new planning tasks.

Abstract KronLSTM introduces a Kronecker Delta Rule for stable associative memory updates in linear attention, improving long-context modeling without extra normalization.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small We propose KronLSTM, a linear attention architecture based on the Kronecker Delta Rule, a novel update rule for associative matrix memories. KronLSTM extends the xLSTM family with a memory-revision mechanism that corrects targeted associations while modifying the memory only along a single direction in matrix space. This minimally invasive memory revision reduces interference with previously stored information compared with delta-rule updates, making it particularly well suited for long-context language modeling. The resulting memory dynamics can be mathematically described as a Markov chain that converges to a steady-state distribution, while the expected norm of the memory matrix remains uniformly bounded throughout the entire input sequence. Hence, KronLSTM ensures stable memory dynamics without requiring additional normalization and stabilization mechanisms for the memory state. Experiments show that KronLSTM is competitive with, and often outperforms, existing linear attention architectures, including models of the (Gated) DeltaNet family, with particularly strong performance on long context benchmarks. These results establish the Kronecker Delta Rule as an effective and analytically tractable foundation for stable associative memory in recurrent linear attention architectures. Our code (https://github.com/NX-AI/KronLSTM) and models (https://huggingface.co/collections/NX-AI/kronlstm) are open-source.

Abstract AdaExploreBench evaluates whether LLM agents can actively discover hidden environmental rules and adapt to unannounced changes, revealing that current models struggle to seek relevant evidence and detect stale knowledge.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in unfamiliar and evolving environments, requiring them to proactively explore, understand, and adapt to environmental dynamics. However, existing benchmarks rarely test whether agents can proactively discover how an environment works and adapt as it evolves. To address this gap, we introduce AdaExploreBench, a benchmark for proactive exploration and test-time adaptation in evolving environments. By instantiating environmental dynamics as hidden rules, AdaExploreBench requires agents to actively acquire evidence and infer the rules. Under implicit rule drift, the latent rule changes without announcement, requiring agents to detect from degraded performance that prior knowledge is stale and re-explore. Experiments on eight leading LLMs show that proactive and adaptive exploration remain challenging: GPT-6 Sol achieves only 61.9% calibrated accuracy, while providing sufficient evidence or removing rule drift improves average performance significantly by close to 10%. These results reveal a critical bottleneck in current LLM agents: they can often reason effectively from informative evidence once it is available, but struggle to actively acquire the right evidence and recognize when previously acquired evidence is no longer valid.

Abstract MemPilot uses reinforcement learning to dynamically curate multimodal agent memory, balancing performance, cost, and latency through adaptive retrieval and model selection.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Memory has become integral to the LLM agent ecosystem, supporting information retention and reuse across interactions. However, most existing agent memory systems construct memory in a query-agnostic manner, which can incur unnecessary preprocessing cost and discard details that later prove essential. Recent studies have begun shifting memory processing toward runtime adaptation, but typically specialize in particular operations or fixed processing schemes, leaving flexible control over performance, cost, and latency largely underexplored. To address this challenge, we present MemPilot, a flexible framework that orchestrates on-demand memory curation under different performance--cost--latency preferences. Specifically, we optimize a multi-step LLM policy via reinforcement learning to iteratively choose between retrieving from query-agnostic memory and delegating query-specific curation of raw multimodal history to heterogeneous LLMs and VLMs. The policy jointly controls evidence amount, curation instructions, model selection, and visual access, enabling fine-grained allocation of runtime computation. To optimize this policy under competing objectives, we adapt objective-wise advantage decoupling by separately estimating each objective's advantage before aggregation. Moreover, we introduce prefix-based marginal utility estimation for fine-grained credit assignment across multi-step rollouts. Experiments on five multimodal agent-memory benchmarks demonstrate favorable performance--cost--latency trade-offs across optimization preferences, with preference sweeps yielding broader frontiers than existing trade-off-aware baselines.

Abstract RoAR-VAE improves visual tokenizers by enforcing robust prediction of semantics and reconstruction from corrupted latents, balancing fidelity with diffusion learnability.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small An effective visual tokenizer must preserve image fidelity while producing latents that downstream diffusion models can learn efficiently. Prior work improves latent diffusability through semantic alignment or joint tokenizer--diffusion training. Revisiting REPA-E, a representative approach, we find that removing its diffusion objective and dedicated generative blocks while retaining semantic prediction from corrupted latents yields comparable downstream generation performance under a matched setting. This observation motivates a general principle of robust prediction: generation-relevant information should remain recoverable from corrupted latents. We instantiate this principle with two complementary objectives. RobustAlign predicts pretrained semantic features from corrupted latents to preserve high-level structure, while RobustRecon reconstructs the clean image to retain fine-grained spatial and appearance information. Together, they form Robust Alignment and Reconstruction VAE (RoAR-VAE). Our analysis suggests that robust prediction improves diffusability by making useful information more accessible in high-variance latent directions, which diffusion models tend to learn earlier. Empirically, RoAR-VAE improves average generation benchmark performance by 2.19 points over the strongest baseline tokenizer under the same training budget, while improving small-text reconstruction accuracy by 16.93 percentage points over FLUX.1-VAE. These results demonstrate that RoAR-VAE achieves a favorable balance between reconstruction fidelity and downstream generative learnability.

Abstract HarnessPro evolves AI agent harnesses without labels to boost task success and lower execution costs, yielding lightweight, balanced, and high-performance variants via trajectory diagnosis and weighted selection.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Harness development plays a crucial role in building capable AI agents. Recent approaches improve harnesses through recursive self-improvement (RSI), but their performance gains often come with higher execution costs. We introduce HarnessPro, a label-free method for evolving harnesses to improve task performance while reducing execution cost. In this setting, candidate harnesses are evaluated using LLM-generated tests without access to ground-truth solutions or official tests. Specialized agents diagnose execution trajectories, plan updates with different performance-cost priorities, and implement them as candidate harnesses. Rather than returning a single harness, HarnessPro uses normalized weighted squared-distance selection to provide cost-focused (lite), balanced (medium), and performance-focused (heavy) harnesses for different deployment budgets. Selected harnesses enter the next iteration, where execution feedback guides further updates. Our experiments cover Terminal-Bench 2, SWE-Bench Pro, and ProgramBench, using GPT-5.5 with Codex and GLM-5.2 with Claude Code. Across these benchmarks using GPT-5.5 with Codex, the heavy mode of HarnessPro achieves an average pass-rate gain of 2.0 percentage points and an average per-task cost reduction of 26.1% relative to RHO. This advantage also holds when using GLM-5.2 with Claude Code. Code is available at https://github.com/pzs19/HarnessPro

Abstract BlenderWorld is a benchmark for evaluating multimodal language models on realistic 3D creation tasks using rendered outputs and binary rubrics, revealing significant gaps in spatial coherence.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Recent advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have enabled them to act as autonomous agents, creating and editing 3D models via code or direct software manipulation. In practical workflows, these models must infer object shapes, materials, spatial layouts, and lighting from diverse references while strictly following instructions to reconstruct, modify, or design 3D content. However, existing benchmarks provide limited coverage of such open-ended requests across real-world scenarios. To bridge this gap, we introduce BlenderWorld, the first comprehensive benchmark dedicated to evaluating frontier models on realistic 3D creation tasks. BlenderWorld comprises 223 tasks spanning architecture, products, natural environments, and scientific visualization, driven by text, image, video, and structured specification inputs. To rigorously assess these complex deliverables, we establish a unified evaluation protocol: we render the submitted Blender projects from multiple viewpoints and evaluate them against 2,651 task-specific binary rubrics alongside holistic visual quality scores. Benchmarking thirteen frontier models reveals that GPT-6-Astra achieves the highest overall fused score of 79.9, followed by Opus-5 (73.0) and GPT-5.6-Sol (67.7). Ultimately, our findings reveal a critical bottleneck: while current models can parse broad instructions, they still profoundly struggle with fine-grained 3D spatial coherence and accurate structural reproduction in Blender, leaving substantial headroom for future 3D-native agentics.

Abstract RoboValue introduces a benchmark to evaluate robotic value models across task-state understanding, progress tracking, failure recovery, and consistency in both simulated and real-world settings.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Robotic value models assess task execution from visual observations and instructions, providing feedback for policy learning and execution monitoring. However, existing evaluations cover only selected aspects of execution understanding, leaving the reliability of value feedback insufficiently tested. We introduce RoboValue, a fine-grained sim-and-real benchmark for unified evaluation of robotic value models. Its framework evaluates four complementary capability dimensions through shared interfaces: task-state understanding, temporal progress monitoring, failure and recovery reasoning, and value consistency. Diagnostic trajectories and instruction variations test whether value judgments accurately reflect task requirements and changes throughout execution. RoboValue-Dataset covers 15 simulation and 20 real-world manipulation tasks, providing expert demonstrations for training and annotated trajectories for fine-grained evaluation. The same semantic subtask can also receive substantially different value gains across valid execution orders. These findings identify concrete targets for value supervision, while the dataset and public leaderboard support continued assessment of feedback reliability. Abstract TCoVP introduces a spatiotemporally grounded video reasoning chain that links entities to timestamped visual evidence for segmentation, improving reasoning and mask prediction across benchmarks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Video Reasoning Segmentation (VRS) requires a model to identify an implicitly described target and predict its pixel-level mask trajectory, while resolving the spatial relations and temporal events that determine the answer. Text-only Chain-of-Thought (CoT) can explain a target choice without making the underlying visual references explicit, leaving a video reference gap between linguistic inference and the evidence distributed across frames. We introduce Temporal Chain of Video Primitives (TCoVP), a spatiotemporally grounded form of Video CoT that organizes query-relevant observations into a chronological reasoning chain. Each primitive associates an entity reference with temporal support and timestamped spatial evidence, and the chain interleaves these references with language to guide reasoning about which entity is relevant, when the queried event holds, and where its evidence appears. The selected target primitives then provide box prompts and temporal support for video mask prediction. We construct TCoVP-SFT-15k and TCoVP-RL-13k to learn this representation through supervised fine-tuning and chain-aligned reinforcement learning. Extensive experiments across five VRS benchmarks and three model backbones demonstrate the strong performance of TCoVP, highlighting the effectiveness of organizing spatial and temporal evidence within the reasoning process. All models and code will be publicly released. Project page: https://victor-wjw.github.io/TCoVP/

Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small In this paper, we systematically investigate the mechanisms, scaling behavior, and generalization of physical Recursive self-improvement. We introduce PhysRSI-eval to quantify physical RSI through a five-axis metric: First-Attempt Performance, Efficiency, Success, Budget and Improvement from failure. Different VLMs are evaluated on 18 manipulation tasks under a fixed interaction budget. The analysis of experimental results motivates the Physical Barrel Hypothesis: different capabilities may constrain different parts of improvement. Abstract Sauron is an evidence-first agentic verifier that independently validates claims against environment state and execution traces to improve rubric-based evaluation accuracy and reduce reinforcement-learning reward errors.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Long-horizon, semi-verifiable agentic tasks often rely on rubric-based evaluation, but the evidence needed to assess these tasks can be spread across the environment state and the execution trace. A judge may overlook this evidence or accept unsupported claims, including an agent's own reports of success. When rubric verdicts serve as reinforcement-learning rewards, such errors can reinforce undesirable behavior. We introduce Sauron, an evidence-first agentic verifier that investigates both state and trace and relies on a claim only after independently validating it against its source. Bounded queries and joint investigation of related criteria reduce context use and redundant work, and each verdict carries an auditable receipt of its supporting evidence. Across 2,586 human-labeled criteria from 100 personal-assistant episodes, Sauron achieves the highest F1 of five verifiers on all eight backbones tested while costing less than Gandalf, the strongest baseline. With GPT-5.6 Sol, it reaches 0.96 F1 versus Gandalf's 0.92 at 23% lower cost, with a wider margin on process criteria. On a paired fault-injection suite covering 13 families, several of which simulate reward hacking, Sauron detects 97% of injected failures while flagging only 2% of clean controls, outperforming baselines even when they are given the full trace. On TRAIL, a benchmark for detecting and localizing real agent errors, Sauron leads both Gandalf and TRAIL's own judge on all three quality metrics.

Abstract AffordanceWAM uses affordance representations from human videos to jointly predict future scenes and robot actions, improving manipulation transfer without human action labels.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Generalizable robot manipulation requires predicting how a scene will evolve, identifying where interactions are feasible, and determining how to act. Action-labeled robot videos directly supervise control but are costly and limited in diversity, whereas egocentric human videos capture diverse interactions but lack robot actions and differ in embodiment and appearance. We introduce AffordanceWAM, an affordance-aware generative World Action Model that represents object-centric spatiotemporal affordance through Scalar Affordance and Affordance Heatmap, within the generated future World. This representation grounds visual prediction in task-relevantant objects and interaction regions for action generation, and provides shared interaction targets across human and robot videos. Built on a pretrained video diffusion Transformer, AffordanceWAM uses separately parameterized World and Action Experts, coupled through Masked Joint Self-Attention, to jointly predict future RGB observations, Scalar Affordance fields, Affordance Heatmaps, and continuous robot actions under a unified flow-matching objective. Human videos supervise all three future-World streams, whereas robot trajectories additionally provide action supervision, enabling transfer without human action labels or retargeting. Experiments on RoboCasa, CALVIN ABCrightarrowD, and real-world manipulation demonstrate consistent gains over RGB-only and robot-data-only baselines. Under fixed robot supervision, RoboCasa performance improves monotonically as affordance-annotated human video scales. These results support affordance as an effective interface for both vision-language-action learning and human-to-robot transfer.

Abstract FineSplat improves feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting by using fine-grained patches, dual grouping for efficient multi-view interaction, rendering-based feedback, and compact Gaussian generation to recover richer geometry and appearance efficiently.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) promises instant scene reconstruction with high-quality novel-view synthesis. Yet, fine-grained geometry and appearance remain difficult to recover, motivating us to revisit how feed-forward models represent, aggregate, and refine detailed visual information. We introduce FineSplat, a feed-forward 3DGS framework built around fine-grained image patches. Since global multi-view attention becomes prohibitively expensive at such high token resolutions, we develop a dual-grouping strategy that combines two complementary forms of locality: view-wise grouping enables efficient interaction among neighboring views, while 3D spatial grouping reconnects geometrically related tokens that may never have interacted under view-wise grouping by lifting them into the 3D scene space. We further leverage reconstruction error as direct feedback, providing an explicit rendering-based signal to recover fine-grained structures and appearance, thereby overcoming the limitations of one-pass feed-forward prediction. Finally, a compact Gaussian generation scheme preserves the benefits of fine-grained features without unnecessarily increasing the number of primitives. Together, these designs enable FineSplat to recover richer geometry and appearance while remaining efficient and compact, achieving state-of-the-art results by a substantial margin, under the same or smaller Gaussian budgets than existing methods.

Abstract Fréchet Forcing fine-tunes streaming video world models using self-generated rollouts and real-data statistics to prevent long-horizon drift without requiring teachers or long videos.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Streaming video world models generate the future chunk by chunk, conditioned on their own past and a stream of actions. Trained on short clips, they drift once a rollout exceeds the training horizon, as prediction errors re-enter the context and compound. A common remedy is to train the model on its own rollouts via distribution matching distillation (DMD). For action-conditioned world models, the teacher becomes a bottleneck: an external video model is agnostic to the actions, whereas the model's own pretrained network drifts in the same manner as the student. We observe that a long rollout need not match a teacher, provided it remains a realistic continuation of its own scene that follows the commanded actions. Realism and scene fidelity can be measured against real-data statistics, which remain valid at any horizon, while action following is preserved by the model's conditional generation loss. We propose Fréchet Forcing, which fine-tunes a world model together with its deployed multi-step sampler under three constraints. Generative anchoring applies the flow-matching loss after a self-generated history ending on real data. Context fidelity, a Fréchet distance (FD) on latent statistics of the free rollout centred on each clip's own ground truth, keeps every rollout faithful to its scene. Reality matching, an FD to real frames in a frozen perceptual space, keeps the generated frames realistic. Because all three targets are derived from short real clips without comparing a rollout against a long recorded future, training requires no teacher, no online critic and no long videos. Fine-tuning MiniWorld-1B, pretrained on 253-frame clips, for at most 36 GPU-hours on 381-frame clips yields rollouts that remain stable beyond 4,000 frames and continue to follow their actions in both robot manipulation and camera-controlled exploration.

Abstract A framework called Stateful Harness separates rule-based world state updates from frozen video rendering to improve consistency in interactive world models with hidden state, evaluated via the WorldState-Bench benchmark.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Existing world models can generate action-conditioned video from a single image. Many focus on camera movement, allowing users to explore the generated scene. However, navigation alone does not ensure that an action follows persistent rules when its outcome depends on state that is invisible in the current frame. We present Stateful Harness, a framework that separates world evolution from video rendering to support stateful and rule-governed interaction. It maintains an evolving world state under specified rules, renders each resulting event with a frozen video model, and uses the generated clip to update the world state for the next turn. At each turn, the harness resolves the action selected by the user and compiles the resulting event into an instruction, while a preview clip from an editable 3D scene provides spatial and camera guidance. To evaluate rule compliance, we build WorldState-Bench, a benchmark of 50 counterfactual twin pairs that share an initial frame and action but differ in hidden state and required outcome. Under rule-only prompting, the best of the 11 evaluated video backends produces the required endings for both cases in 32% of pairs. For MiniMax-H3, specifying the required endings raises this rate from 32% to 64%, while the full harness reaches 92% with the same frozen model. These results support separating rule execution from video rendering for world interactions with hidden state.

Abstract Self-forcing training with teacher distillation and asymmetric conditioning stabilizes multi-turn image editing against accumulating distribution shifts.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Image editing models produce impressive results for individual edits, but their quality often deteriorates over multiple editing turns. Each editing turn introduces small distribution shifts that accumulate over successive edits, creating a gap between the input distributions encountered during the editor's original training and multi-turn inference. We introduce a training scheme that makes multi-turn image editing stable under this accumulating distribution shift. A key is to force the editor to operate on its own outputs from previous editing turns during training, bringing the principle of Self-Forcing to image editing. The main challenge is that self-generated editing trajectories deviate from ground-truth trajectories, making direct supervision unreliable. We address this challenge through a distillation objective that leverages a teacher image editor to provide a reliable training signal when the model operates on its own previous outputs. We introduce an asymmetric conditioning scheme: while the student performs edits sequentially on its own outputs, the teacher remains anchored to the original clean image and receives a compound editing instruction. Experiments show substantially reduced degradation over long multi-turn sequences while maintaining editing quality and instruction adherence.

Abstract MARS enables multimodal safety steering using only text-derived refusal directions, avoiding the need for multimodal safety data.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Safety in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) depends on unsafe multimodal data for post-training alignment or activation steering. However, such data is hard to collect, must be gathered anew for each modality, and its collection and distribution raise ethical and legal concerns. In this work, we relax this constraint and investigate whether textual refusal directions, extracted directly from the LLM backbone, generalize across modalities (i.e., image, video). Preliminary findings confirm this ability, though effectiveness is conditioned by layer selection, steering strength, and cross-modal alignment, with the latter causing safe multimodal inputs to be spuriously steered toward refusal. MARS corrects modality misalignment via activation centering, adaptively scales steering strength within a geometrically defined trust region, and selects the optimal intervention layer, operating at the first generated token. Evaluated on five SOTA MLLMs across safety, utility, and video jailbreak, MARS achieves consistent safety gains while preserving utility up to over-refusal. MARS reveals that safety-relevant structure is shared across modalities and that textual refusal directions are a powerful and underexplored foundation for multimodal alignment.

Abstract TokenWorth is a lightweight online memory framework that learns which past experiences are worth retaining and reusing under tight resource constraints, improving lifelong agent success while reducing token usage.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Lifelong agents continually accumulate and reuse experience to improve after deployment, yet in practice they often operate under tight generation, interaction, and memory budgets. Under such stringent joint constraints, we find that existing lifelong-memory methods can become ineffective, with several sophisticated approaches offering little advantage over simple retrieval or even no-memory agents. This reveals a more fundamental problem: resource scarcity makes each memory decision more consequential. When only a small amount of past experience can be retained or reused and opportunities for reasoning and recovery are limited, retrieving a relevant but ineffective memory can consume scarce context and hinder downstream execution. The key challenge is therefore not simply to retrieve relevant experience, but to determine which experiences are actually worth retaining and reusing. To address this challenge, we propose TokenWorth, a lightweight online memory framework that learns query-dependent memory worth from sparse execution feedback and applies it consistently to memory retrieval and bounded memory management. Across three LifelongAgentBench environments and both local and API-based deployments, TokenWorth achieves the highest task success rate while using the fewest output tokens among the evaluated methods. These results suggest that effective lifelong adaptation under resource constraints depends not on remembering more, but on learning what is worth remembering. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/AIGNLAI/TokenWorth.

Abstract SERA trains recursive language agents to generate evaluation rubrics for subtasks, improving decomposition, solving, and self-evaluation with reduced external supervision.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Recursive language-model agents decompose tasks and delegate subtasks to child instances of the same policy, forming a tree of work. Training them, however, is hard: the final outcome is verifiable, but the self-invented intermediate subtasks are numerous and carry no ground truth. Existing methods score each node with a verifier or judge, which is costly at scale and blind to decomposition quality. We argue that a recursive agent must learn three coupled capabilities within one set of weights: decomposing problems into subtasks, solving them, and evaluating the outcomes, each requiring its own training signal. SERA (Self-Evaluating Recursive Agents) turns evaluation into a learned capability of the policy itself. Before delegating, the parent writes a rubric of weighted success criteria for each child subtask; a ranking objective against verified outcomes then trains rubric generation so that the criteria track genuine subtask success. In addition, a complementary leaf-coverage signal provides direct credit for task decomposition. Our central finding is that training the policy to generate aligned rubrics is what drives the gains: because the same weights both evaluate and execute, learning to judge subtasks sharpens the agent's ability to solve them. Notably, external supervision is also reduced: the judge is consulted only to train the rubric generator, while solving is trained against the agent's own rubric scores, which outperform direct use of the judge. Beyond training, the learned rubric doubles as an inference-time selector for tree search. On TextCraft-Synth and TextWorld-Sync, SERA improves over strong recursive-agent baselines by 5.38 and 13.14 points on average, and rubric-guided tree search at inference adds a further 2.43 points on TextWorld-Sync.
