Abstract Guardrail benchmark scores primarily reflect labeling policy rather than detection ability, with minor parameter changes shifting results across full ranges and training contamination undermining reported safety metrics.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small A guardrail classifier is reported as if unsafe were a property of text. It is not: it is a property of a policy, and the policy enters the model through its labels. We exploit an accident of an industrial deployment in which the same ~53k prompts were labelled twice, under two operational definitions ("is this a request for harmful content?" and "would a moderator block this?"), and used to train ten guardrail arms sharing architecture, codebase and optimiser family, at two model scales. First, the guardrail's own training corpus already contained the benchmarks: JailbreakBench-benign is 88% and OR-Bench-hard 82% present in the training pools, two over-refusal benchmarks, the kind a guardrail reports to argue it does not over-block. Everything below is decontaminated. Second, safety benchmarks stratify sharply by how much of their score is decided by the label definition rather than by the model: with architecture and optimiser fixed, the range across the ten arms is 0.99 on HarmBench-copyright and 0.043 on WildGuard-benign, a 23x spread. Third, a benchmark score can be moved across its entire range by 0.03% of the parameters. Across these arms HarmBench-copyright spans 0-100 and OR-Bench-hard over-refusal 3.4-56.4%, and the four arms whose last supervision signal carries a given policy occupy one extreme of both (exact rank p=0.005, or 0.04-0.14 at worst), at unchanged detection capability. For a distilled student the teacher's soft labels do this even when every hard label carries the other policy, so teacher provenance is an unrecorded confound in any released checkpoint. Fourth, validation accuracy on the one split that grades all twenty arms tracks agreement with the annotator's policy (rho=+0.74), not detection capability (rho=+0.34). We argue that guardrail evaluations should report contamination, a seed floor and the definition they were graded against as first-class variables.

Abstract ECHO integrates multi-horizon prediction into the backbone via self-recursion and dynamic depth sampling, improving pretraining efficiency and inference drafting without auxiliary modules.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Existing Multi-token prediction (MTP) methods are auxiliary by design: the multi-horizon loss mostly trains modules attached to the backbone, and each added horizon costs another module and another pass. We propose Efficient Causal Self-Recursion (ECHO), which removes both costs. The backbone's own final layer predicts every horizon, so the supervision lands on the parameters that generate the next token, and Dynamic Depth Sampling (DDS) keeps deeper horizons affordable by drawing the training depth with probabilities that decay along depth. Future prediction thus becomes part of the autoregressive objective rather than a task attached to it, and the gains follow on both sides of it. In pretraining, added depth converts into downstream accuracy where auxiliary modules saturate, concentrating on reasoning and code at half the MTP training overhead of training every depth. At inference, the pretrained model is its own drafter: it drafts beyond the depth it was trained for, and keeps its draft-target alignment on the open-ended, high-entropy generation where post-hoc drafters lose theirs. The advantage survives a doubled token budget, a repeated pretraining run, and supervised finetuning.

Abstract Autoregressive transformers retain prompt influence via a power-law decay driven by attention dilution and relay mechanisms, with trained models forgetting faster than untrained ones.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small We ask how much an autoregressive transformer retains of its prompt after generating t tokens. Consider two different prompts followed by the same continuation text, so that any difference in their hidden states comes from the prompt alone, and we find that the distance between their hidden states decays approximately as a power law t^{-α}, where α=1 is the rate at which a fixed prompt's share of uniform attention shrinks and smaller values mean longer memory. Trained models forget fast (αapprox0.55--0.7), untrained ones slowly (αapprox0.2--0.5). We prove that this offset is Obig(P(log t)^{L-1}/tbig) for a prompt of length P and depth L. The bound holds for finite-depth pre-LN softmax transformers with bounded attention logits, under one additional condition: the pre-norm activations stay away from zero. The mechanism is geometric: the prompt's direct influence is diluted as the context grows, and relay --- continuation tokens passing on what earlier tokens read from the prompt --- can only add logarithmic factors. This is what we call the conditional no-hair theorem: given the text generated so far, the prompt's influence on the next token vanishes as t grows, so any property of the prompt that survives, such as its language, must be carried by the text itself. An exactly solvable linear relay model shows that the bound cannot be improved and reproduces the rise of the local exponent towards 1 seen in untrained networks. Cancelling the dilution with a logit bias stops the decay early in training and reverses it after, so dilution is necessary for the forgetting we measure; untrained networks and the solvable model show it is also sufficient.

Abstract UniAVC trains separate audio and video branches on abundant unimodal data before aligning them with scarce paired data, using independent noise schedules and asymmetric supervision to enable joint synthesis, cross-modal generation, and instruction-guided editing.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Unified audio-video creation requires a single model capable of text-conditioned joint synthesis, bidirectional cross-modal generation, and instruction-guided editing across both modalities. Existing methods rely heavily on paired corpora, which are scarce for joint generation and even rarer for editing. Consequently, they are spent learning single-branch generative and editing capabilities that abundant unimodal data could readily teach, rather than on the cross-modal coupling. Furthermore, prevailing systems apply a single shared noise schedule to both modalities, neglecting two critical limitations. First, due to the significant discrepancy in information density, video requires prolonged high noise to organize spatial and motion structure, whereas the sparser audio signal can settle earlier during the synthesis process, the dynamics a uniform schedule might compromise. Second, lockstep denoising leaves asymmetric workflows, such as conditioning on one modality to synthesize or edit another, untrained. We present UniAVC, which addresses both obstacles. It first trains each branch on unimodal dataset to full generative and editing competence, and subsequently dedicates paired data to exploit cross-modal correspondence. Modality-decoupled schedulers meanwhile warp the noise schedule independently from a shared timestep, while subtasks with one clean modality directly supervise asymmetric configurations. These settings respectively enable intent-aware editing, which preserves one modality intact while enlisting it to constrain the other, and post-inference self-refinement, which regenerates only an unsatisfactory branch against an accepted one. UniAVC thus spans multiple creation tasks from a single set of weights without task-specific adapters.

Abstract Hybrid language models suffer from competing learning between recurrence and attention layers, and a staged training procedure improves retrieval and overall performance.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Many modern language models have adopted hybrid architectures that incorporate both linear-time recurrence and softmax attention. Modern recurrence layers provide a highly expressive mixing mechanism while ensuring high throughput by compressing contextual information into a fixed-size state, but underperform softmax attention in contextual information retrieval. In this paper, we reveal a Competing Learning phenomenon where different kinds of mixers compete to learn the same function, and when any layer learns a function, it suppresses the corresponding learning signal for other layers. Layer attribution analysis reveals that under typical data distributions, modern recurrence learns retrieval functions faster than softmax attention, causing attention to fail to learn retrieval in most cases. Stopping weight updates in recurrence in the middle of training helps attention learn retrieval. Based on these results, we propose a staged training procedure for pre-training hybrid architectures on natural language. Empirical results on models up to 1B parameters show that our method results in substantial improvement in language modeling loss, common-sense reasoning, as well as a wide range of retrieval-related tasks across different context lengths.

Abstract DScout improves speculative decoding throughput by aligning diffusion drafter training and inference with prefix acceptance and latency trade-offs.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference through parallel verification of tokens proposed by a drafter. Diffusion drafters further parallelize proposal generation, but greater proposal capacity or higher mean accepted tokens (MAT) need not yield higher throughput. Because verification accepts only a consecutive prefix, early rejections can render later correct predictions ineffective, limiting MAT gains, while even higher MAT may not improve throughput if per-round latency increases. We introduce DScout, which uses two complementary factorizations to guide diffusion drafter training and inference for higher throughput. For training, DScout factorizes position-wise loss into inclusion, weighting, and prediction loss. This guides the design and combination of mixed-horizon training, intra-block unmasking, and acceptance-weighted loss to align supervision with prefix acceptance and improve MAT. For inference, DScout factorizes throughput as the ratio of MAT to per-round latency, guiding target-conditioning depth, drafter depth, and inference block size selection to identify a high-throughput configuration for each workload. Across six benchmarks, DScout achieves 3.84times and 4.14times mean speedups over autoregressive decoding on Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B, respectively, improving mean speedup over the state-of-the-art baseline by 9.1% and 7.1%. Across models and concurrency settings, our inference configuration policy provides up to 9.75% additional mean speedup over DScout's fixed configuration.

Abstract CoPE improves agentic reinforcement learning by training policies to generate multi-step action sequences from trajectory histories, increasing sample efficiency without inference overhead.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Training language agents is bottlenecked by the high cost of long-horizon rollouts, yet standard agentic reinforcement learning (RL) learns only from a terminal scalar reward, leaving the supervision in intermediate steps largely unused. Prior work exploits this supervision through value targets that estimate per-step success probabilities or observation targets that fit environment response. Both are hard to construct well: (1) value targets come from the same single scalar signal for each step in the trajectory, and (2) observation targets, such as web pages and terminal logs, are long and noisy and reach only one step ahead. We propose CoPE, which adds a third kind of multi-step action targets. On successful on-policy trajectories, CoPE trains the policy backbone to generate the action sequences that follows each history. Each action is also supervised when forecast from the preceding histories, forming a temporal ensemble that the shared backbone absorbs during training, and this enables acting and forecasting to co-evolve. The policy still acts one step at a time at inference, with no overhead. Across five benchmarks (ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, WebShop, τ^2-bench, and AppWorld), CoPE (1) achieves stronger aggregate performance than GRPO and RL baselines with predictive auxiliary targets across simulated environments, while improving overall performance on both realistic applications; (2) delivers up to 1.9times and 2.0times higher sample efficiency than ECHO and GRPO, respectively, on ScienceWorld; and (3) achieves these gains with a single policy backbone, without additional models, separate pretraining, or inference overhead. The codebase is available at https://github.com/ulab-uiuc/CoPE.

Abstract StopLoss is a lightweight external monitor that predicts coding-agent failures from recent tool steps to abort doomed runs early, reducing compute costs while preserving successful outcomes.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Coding agents keep working on runs that will fail verification, and asking a frontier model to watch for this is expensive in its own right. We introduce StopLoss, a small external monitor that learns failure recognition from completed trajectories and their verifier outcomes. The name is inspired by the stop-loss order in finance: StopLoss ends a run early when the run is likely to fail, which saves resources. Final outcomes label earlier local windows, so a fine-tuned language model can score the most recent tool steps in a single forward pass, and a threshold calibrated on successful runs turns that score into an abort decision without changing the executor. Trained on GPT-family SWE-rebench trajectories and evaluated on SWE-bench Verified and a SWE-bench Pro subset with GPT and Claude executors, StopLoss is compared against six general-purpose judges reading identical windows. Outcome supervision lifts failure prediction from near chance to a useful signal at the first check, at one to two orders of magnitude lower cost per check than any tested judge. This translates into savings: relative to completing every task, StopLoss lowers cost per retained solve by 4.5\% on Verified while preserving 99\% of successful runs, and by up to 31\% on the costlier Pro subset, reaching 48\% with a Qwen3-8B backbone, and in all four settings its cost per retained solve is below that of every judge policy. Outcome-trained small monitors can make failure recognition cheap enough for early stopping to pay for itself.

Abstract Progress-Aware Distillation detects and penalizes stagnation in distilled small language model agents to improve reasoning efficiency and success rates.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small While LLM-based agent systems have demonstrated remarkable proficiency in reasoning and solving complex real-world tasks, they also come with substantial token consumption and inference latency, limiting their practical deployment. To enhance the efficiency of agent systems, recent efforts have focused on transferring agentic capabilities from large-scale models to small language models (SLMs) via supervised fine-tuning-based distillation. Nevertheless, as interaction trajectories grow longer, involving extended reasoning-action chains and extensive environmental feedback, distilled student agents tend to experience progress stagnation: becoming trapped in unproductive loops characterized by persistent failed strategies and difficulty making meaningful progress. Our systematic analysis shows that this phenomenon consistently occurs across diverse SLMs and task domains. To address this issue, we propose Progress-Aware Distillation (ProD), which explicitly detects and penalizes stagnation in student-generated trajectories. ProD encodes the stagnation gap between student and teacher into an adaptive margin for dynamically penalizing possible stagnation. By iteratively applying this procedure, ProD enables student agents to progressively approximate teacher behavior. Extensive experiments involving 8 SLMs, ranging from 0.6B to 8B parameters, on both in-domain and out-of-domain benchmarks, demonstrate that ProD substantially mitigates progress stagnation while enhancing both the success rates and task-completion efficiency of distilled SLM agents.

Abstract Conditional Program compresses language-model embedding tables by reconstructing only requested rows via shared codes and local scales, reducing storage by 75% while improving fidelity.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Conditional memory expands language-model capacity through selective lookup, but the full embedding table must remain available for every possible input. Abstract On-policy self-distillation with privileged information improves multi-turn agent performance depending on content and source, and KL-based metrics better predict training gains than direct utility estimates.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) enables large language models (LLMs) to learn from privileged information (PI) without a separate teacher model. However, prior work largely treats PI as a fixed design choice, leaving the effects of its content and construction poorly understood. This challenge is particularly important in multi-turn agentic settings, where trajectories contain actions, observations, and reasoning that can be transformed into PI in many ways. We systematically evaluate 16 PI configurations across three multi-turn agentic benchmarks and three student models that vary in scale and reasoning mode. We find that PI effectiveness depends on its content, generation source, and the student model. Information-dense trajectories and PI produced by more capable generators generally yield larger gains, whereas thinking models benefit less consistently. To assess PI before training, we compare PI utility with two KL-based metrics: sampled-token reverse KL (D) and error-step KL contrast (Δd^{-}_{err}). Empirically, selecting each task's PI by Δd^{-}_{err} also improves post-training performance over random selection, most when the PI candidates differ widely. Finally, effective PI remains beneficial when combined with GRPO, allowing the combined objective to outperform a strong GRPO baseline. Overall, our results provide practical guidance for constructing and evaluating PI in OPSD training.

Abstract Halcyon demonstrates that strong text-to-image generation can be achieved by training multimodal autoregressive models from scratch on image-text pairs without relying on large-scale language pretraining. Abstract Hierarchical Score Posterior Recovery improves agent evaluation efficiency by combining historical response patterns with adaptive task selection to estimate full benchmark scores from small subsets.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Evaluating software agents on a full benchmark is expensive. A small task subset can preserve agent rankings while yielding inaccurate scores. Historical responses provide useful structure, but tasks with identical source responses can have different outcomes for a new target agent. We introduce Hierarchical Score Posterior Recovery (HSPR), which combines source-fitted response factors with uncertain group success rates. Observed target outcomes update the posteriors over the target latent state and group rates; posterior-guided acquisition selects subsequent tasks. Its score-based criterion equals the expected one-step reduction in full-score posterior variance. Observed successes and posterior-predictive counts for unqueried tasks yield a full-score posterior. The final estimate uses its median with source-selected calibration. At a 10% task budget on four task banks from the SWE-bench family, HSPR achieves the lowest mean absolute error (MAE) on three banks and the lowest maximum absolute error (MaxErr) on all four among the methods compared. Removing group uncertainty or replacing adaptive queries increases macro MAE in the respective controls. Macro MAE decreases across 5-20% budgets.

Abstract ActionRoPE encodes action-driven camera motion into world-coordinate rotary positions for consistent spatial control, paired with coordinate-conditioned noise scheduling to stabilize observed regions before generating new content.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Game world models have made rapid progress toward real-time interactive generation. Yet action control is still predominantly implemented as feature conditioning, leaving the model to learn from training data how each action should move the visual world. This formulation is poorly matched to a broad class of planar-camera worlds, such as top-down and isometric games. In these worlds, player motion induces a geometrically well-defined planar camera displacement. We observe that the key to spatially consistent control in such worlds is to represent the effect of each action directly in world coordinates. To this end, we introduce ActionRoPE, a parameter-free action interface that converts action-driven camera displacement into world-coordinate rotary positions. As a result, tokens corresponding to the same world location retain the same positional address across time. These world coordinates further expose a spatial asymmetry in generation: regions covered by the initial observation are geometrically known, while newly revealed regions remain to be synthesized. We therefore propose Coordinate-Conditioned Noise Scheduling (CNS), which assigns token-wise denoising schedules according to this known-new structure. It allows observed regions to stabilize earlier, while new content is generated with reference to them. Compared with four representative action-conditioning mechanisms, our approach follows the commanded motion more faithfully and reduces the revisit drift by 66% over the strongest baseline. The same interface transfers across video backbones from 1.3B to 33B parameters and to out-of-distribution scenes. Project Page: https://teawhite.cn/ActionRoPE/

Abstract Researchers identify state hallucination in vision-language-action models and propose sparse feature unlearning to reduce failure behaviors while preserving manipulation skills.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have shown strong generalization in robotic manipulation by leveraging rich representations from pretrained vision-language models. However, their deployment in real-world environments remains limited by recurring unreliable behaviors. In this work, we study state hallucination, a recurring failure pattern in which a VLA continues acting as if an unrealized robot-object state had been achieved. Our analyses find that state hallucination coincides with weakened attention to task-relevant visual regions, and a mechanistic interpretation via sparse autoencoders reveals that hallucination-associated sparse features are activated when these failures occur. Experiments across VLA architectures in simulated and real-world environments show that our method substantially reduces hallucinated failures and improves task success without substantially compromising the existing manipulation capabilities. These results suggest that interpretable feature analysis provides a practical basis for selectively modifying undesirable knowledge in robot policies.

Abstract EC2-Bench evaluates edge-cloud collaboration for long-horizon LLM agents, revealing trade-offs among performance, resource use, and privacy, and showing that stronger edge models favor more edge-controlled collaboration.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Edge-cloud collaboration combines the lower inference overhead and greater data locality of edge-side execution with the stronger capabilities of cloud-hosted models. However, as LLM agents increasingly operate in long-horizon scenarios, cloud participation becomes interleaved throughout execution rather than a one-shot decision for each query. Such interleaved participation introduces trajectory-level effects that remain insufficiently understood. To address this gap, we introduce EC2-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating edge-cloud collaboration in long-horizon agent execution. It provides executable digital-workspace agent tasks and a unified evaluation framework covering task performance, resource usage, and privacy exposure. Using this benchmark, we empirically study diverse forms of edge-cloud collaboration. Our study reveals three findings. First, collaboration achieves a favorable balance among task performance, resource usage, and privacy exposure compared with edge-only and cloud-only execution. Second, as edge models become stronger, collaborative execution approaches cloud-only performance, and performance differences across strategies shrink, while privacy exposure remains strongly strategy-dependent. Third, stronger edge capability shifts preferred collaboration from externally controlled cloud participation and direct cloud execution toward more edge-controlled collaboration. Further analyses identify cloud-invocation decision-making and instruction following as important edge-side abilities for effective collaboration. These findings provide a systematic understanding of edge-cloud collaboration in long-horizon agent execution and insights for designing future edge agents that effectively collaborate with cloud models. Code and resources are available at https://github.com/OpenBMB/EC2-Bench.

Abstract HACE is a framework that jointly evolves specialized foundation models and LLM agents through reciprocal learning and evolutionary optimization of their interaction harness, improving performance across recommendation, protein prediction, and materials discovery.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Recent advances in self-improving LLM agents have demonstrated the potential of AI systems that drive their own intelligence. Extending recursive self-improvement to these models presents a key challenge: as a specialist improves, the strategies used to advance it must also adapt. To address this challenge, we propose Heterogeneous Agentic CoEvolution (HACE), a framework that jointly evolves specialized foundation models and LLM agents through reciprocal learning. HACE improves the specialist through supervised learning while training the agent with on-policy reinforced self-training, guided by verified performance gains on the current specialist. To sustain this reciprocal learning, HACE further employs evolutionary search to optimize their interaction harness, adapting how the models exchange information and select training experiences as their capabilities evolve. We evaluate HACE across three challenging domains: recommendation systems, protein function prediction in biology, and crystal stability prediction in material discovery. We show that HACE outperforms the evaluated self-improving methods on the primary task metrics across all three domains. These results shed light on heterogeneous coevolution as a universal approach to improving specialized foundation models through reciprocal interaction with a learning agent.

Abstract HERA co-evolves agent harnesses and task environments to improve abstention and task completion across diverse LLMs without model-specific tuning.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly capable of acting in complex tool-use environments, yet they often fail to recognize when tasks are infeasible and no valid solution exists. Recent work has formalized this reliability gap as the problem of agentic abstention, and existing approaches typically optimize a model or agent harness against a fixed set of tasks, leading to limited generalization to unseen failure modes. We introduce HERA, a framework for harness-environment co-evolution for agentic abstention. HERA consists of (i) a pipeline to automatically construct verifiable pairs of feasible and infeasible tasks by applying controlled environment mutations that transform solvable tasks into cases requiring abstention, and (ii) a co-evolution procedure in which performance failures on previous tasks are used to drive harness adaptation and generate new execution environments and tasks geared towards previous weaknesses. On held-out evaluation tasks, an evolved harness from HERA improves abstention accuracy from 61.7% to 83.3% while improving feasible-task completion from 68.3% to 76.7%, achieving the highest abstention and feasible-task completion among the compared methods. The resulting best harness transfers across 19 other LLMs, improving abstention accuracy by 15.3 percentage points on average without any model-specific optimization, and enabling smaller models to match the performance of more powerful models at an estimated 85% lower cost.

Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small One of the first steps of a research pipeline is generating ideas. Given prior papers, a model must propose a set of ideas that are each high-quality and clearly distinct from one another, while also being grounded in the input documents. To optimize for this, we propose training with a set-level reinforcement learning objective. In a pilot human study with four experts, our sets are judged as promising as Claude Opus 4.8's and contain marginally more unique ideas. When a coding agent runs proof-of-concept experiments on the ideas, our model's success@k is comparable to Claude Opus 4.8's, so we see that the added diversity does not cost any executability.

Abstract CyberArena is a dynamic adversarial benchmark that evaluates AI agents through concurrent multi-round attack-and-defense combat in evolving environments, measuring capabilities via a dynamical scoring model.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small AI agents are demonstrating increasingly strong offensive and defensive cybersecurity capabilities in the real world. Current benchmarks mostly evaluate agents in isolation against static targets. Real cyber operations, however, are dynamic and coupled: security postures constantly change as attackers and defenders adapt to each other in real time. Static benchmarks quickly saturate and miss an agent's ability to attack and defend simultaneously under live pressure. To address this gap, we present CyberArena, a dynamic, symmetric adversarial benchmark built around multi-round attack-and-defense combat. In isolated sandboxes, two agents compete under identical rules, acting concurrently as both attackers and defenders over continuously changing services. This converts static targets into evolving, semi-open challenges, directly testing an agent's speed and precision in discovering, exploiting, and patching vulnerabilities. To sustain the benchmark's longevity, an automated agentic pipeline continuously generates, reviews, and validates new environments. Furthermore, because pairwise contests are notoriously hard to quantify, we introduce a dynamical model that maps match dynamics into unified, multidimensional scores across offensive, defensive, and domain-specific capabilities. Evaluating 12 frontier LLM agents across 40 challenges in 466 matches, CyberArena separates model capabilities where single-agent baselines hit a ceiling. The trajectories read like a fight rather than an exam: defenders patch one hole and miss the next and certify repairs on stale evidence, while capable agents think a move ahead, planting backdoors for later rounds and, in one case, writing their own packet sniffer to watch the opponent. Beyond a benchmark, CyberArena serves as an adaptable, scalable experimental platform for future research on cybersecurity agents.

Abstract Evaluation readout choices for vision-language models on the POPE benchmark cause large F1 shifts, revealing that unreported token lookup protocols and quantization confound reported hallucination-mitigation gains.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small We set out to reproduce a recent hallucination-mitigation result on the POPE benchmark. The LLaVA-1.5-7B baseline we measured, 82.21 F1 on the adversarial split, sat 1.21 to 8.67 points above the adversarial baselines we planned to compare against (81.00, 79.30, and 73.54). We looked at the evaluation code and found a readout choice that moves F1 on its own. A two-token logit readout decides yes versus no by comparing two vocabulary IDs, 3582 ("yes") and 1217 ("no"). Across 9,000 greedy-decode questions spanning all three splits, neither ID is generated even once. The model emits " Yes" (token 3869) and " No" (token 1939), because SentencePiece prepends a space byte after the prompt suffix "ASSISTANT:". Read with an eight-token lookup that covers the space-prefixed surface forms, LLaVA-1.5-7B scores F1 0.8221, 0.8498, and 0.8713 on the adversarial, popular, and random splits. That 6.13-point adversarial shift exceeds the adversarial gain reported by five of the six papers we surveyed. None of them states its readout, and our reference ran under 4-bit NF4 quantization, so we cannot say how much of any reported gain a matched evaluation would keep. That comparison stays unresolved until readout and precision are matched. On the fixed baseline we run nine inference-time corrections of our own (none beat it) and dissect why the adversarial split resists logit-level intervention. In a 3,000-question probe, wrong predictions draw more image attention than correct ones (Cohen's d=-0.46, p=1.4times10^{-24}), the opposite of what grounding-based corrections assume. A four-readout audit across four further VLMs from three tokenizer families confirms the confound is protocol-level, not model-specific: porting the LLaVA-1.5 IDs verbatim drops F1 to 0.00 on the two disjoint-vocabulary models, whereas a tokenizer-derived readout holds between 0.80 and 0.92.

Abstract VLDA reformulates robotic control as compact transition modeling via a Delta variable that bridges vision-language understanding and action generation, improving long-horizon consistency and generalization.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Robotic manipulation is not merely observation-to-action generation, but the realization of task-specified state changes in the physical world. Existing vision-language-action (VLA) methods directly learn observation-to-action mappings, imposing a substantial learning burden due to the gap between semantic understanding and action execution. Although world-action models (WAMs) improve control through future prediction, modeling complete future states wastes capacity on static appearance and action-irrelevant changes. We propose Vision-Language-Delta-Action (VLDA), a paradigm that reformulates full future prediction as compact actionable transition modeling. Its core is a compact Delta variable that captures latent world-state changes, serving as an interface between vision-language understanding and action generation. VLDA learns Delta through a unified objective composed of the Local Transition Filter (LT-Filter) and Global Transition Anchor (GT-Anchor). LT-Filter captures task-relevant manipulation-centric changes while suppressing static reconstruction biases. GT-Anchor provides coarse future-level regularization to preserve the overall direction of state changes. Delta-Driven Action Refinement (DDAR) further grounds Delta in the action manifold and injects it as a structured seed to guide action denoising. We implement VLDA within LoongForge, an open-source training framework for large-scale foundation and embodied models, using its modular and distributed infrastructure for unified two-stage optimization. Experiments show that VLDA improves long-horizon consistency, robustness, and generalization, validating actionable Delta as an alternative to direct action prediction and full future-state modeling.

Abstract QD-Rec uses quality-diversity optimization and foundation models to generate diverse, well-reasoned recommendations for high-stakes decision-making.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Open-ended search combined with foundation models has produced advances such as faster algorithms, new results on open mathematical problems, and self-improving agents. We extend this paradigm to recommendation for decision-making, focusing on decisions where the set of possible choices is fixed but the reasons for choosing them are not. In high-stakes decisions, e.g., which company to acquire or which customer to target, comparing diverse, well-reasoned options can be more useful than a single ranked list. We formalize open-ended recommendation as building a diverse, high-quality set of recommendations, each an item together with a hypothesis, a natural-language reason why the item fits the needs of a decision maker. We propose QD-Rec, a quality--diversity method that selects items with a contextual bandit and generates hypotheses with a foundation model from earlier ones kept in an archive as stepping stones. A new recommendation enters the archive if it is novel or better than its nearest neighbor. Across M&A, customer targeting, and paper and book recommendation, QD-Rec gives the highest mean quality and builds the largest archive in most settings.

Abstract LILIE uses an invertible normalizing-flow encoder to derive language-informed supervision for text-to-image generation, improving fidelity over pixel-only training.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small End-to-end visual generation with pixel-space loss has recently achieved competitive performance without the need for learned image tokenizers. In this work, we study whether language-informed supervision can provide a richer training signal than pixel loss for visual generation. We introduce LILIE (pronounced "lily"), a Language-Informed and Lossless Image Encoder. LILIE is an invertible image transform trained such that a vision-language model can recover captions from noised features. The encoder uses a normalizing flow transformer trained jointly with a vision-language model via next token prediction. We then use the encoder to extract a simple language-informed loss for text-to-image generation. We train JiT models with LILIE loss, reducing gFD by 14 points (66.84 to 53.01) against a pixel-only baseline on the GPIC dataset. Our code, encoders, and generative models are available at https://suchiragarwal.com/lilie.

Abstract Mechanistic uncertainty is quantified via internal mechanism stability using causal robustness margins and concept steering vectors, revealing that incorrect responses are more sensitive to interventions and that learned facts gain robustness.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in critical domains, where their susceptibility to hallucinations makes reliable and interpretable uncertainty quantification essential. We introduce the concept of mechanistic uncertainty, which characterizes uncertainty through the stability of the internal mechanisms supporting a model's response. We propose the causal robustness margin, defined as the minimum internal intervention required to deviate the model's output, and operationalize it using Jacobian Lens (Jlens) concept steering vectors. Across three open-source models and five question-answering datasets, we show incorrect and uncertain responses exhibit smaller margins, indicating greater sensitivity to internal interventions, and the causal robustness margin complements conventional output-based uncertainty methods. Finally, in a controlled knowledge injection experiment, correctly learned facts show increased margins after training, suggesting that causal robustness provides a mechanistically grounded signal of uncertainty.

Abstract Direct diffusion modeling in a 3D latent space improves spatial consistency and efficiency for scene-level 3D generation compared to 2D-based methods.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Scene-level 3D generation has long been dominated by 2D multi-view or video diffusion models. Typically, these 2D-based approaches perform scene generation in a 2D latent space, which introduces two fundamental issues: (i) representing 3D scenes via 2D views leads to significant representation redundancy, and (ii) latent space rooted in 2D inherently limits the spatial consistency of the generated scenes. In this paper, we propose to perform 3D scene generation directly within a high-dimensional implicit 3D latent space derived from powerful 2D foundation models (e.g., DINOv2, SigLIP2, DA3). Then we tame diffusion transformers to perform diffusion modeling directly in the 3D latent space, enabling 3D-grounded scene generation. In our experiments, we not only demonstrate the advantages of our method over 2D-based approaches in terms of efficiency and spatial consistency, but also comprehensively analyze the impact of 3D latent representation on 3D scene generation performance. Furthermore, we validate the favorable scalability of our method with respect to representation and model capacity.

Abstract  Agent skills are Markdown documents that a tool-using agent reads as instructions and then acts on with the privileges of the session that loaded them. Installing one grants authority (the standing to direct the agent, not new privilege), and the size of that grant is what we measure. We present a two-phase attack-surface profiling methodology that combines whole-bundle static analysis and dependency provenance checking with instrumented sandbox execution and separate model-based assessment of the resulting traces. We apply the methodology to 6,309 listings from two marketplaces, selecting listings for execution without conditioning that decision on a prior threat or risk signal. Half the corpus carries evidence of shell-execution capability; the corresponding shares are 35% for filesystem read, 31% each for filesystem write and network egress, and 13% for credential access; seven listings in ten carry at least one elevated capability. That is the surface a user accepts at install time, and almost none of it is visible then. A permission model exists but is close to inert. Authors may list the tools a skill pre-approves in its own frontmatter, which removes confirmation prompts rather than restricting what the skill can reach; of the 6,061 listings whose frontmatter parses, 770 declare anything at all, the vocabulary cannot express credential or environment access, and nothing checks what it does express against the bundle. Declaring is also not declaring accurately: 59% of declaring listings understate what their bundle can do. ClawHub declares roughly twice as often as skillsmp, yet under-declares on 88% of its declarations against 31%. Triage at the marketplace boundary therefore cannot rest on what a listing declares. It must rest on what the bundle can do. These results position capability-oriented attack-surface profiling as a necessary complement to maliciousness detection.

Abstract Transcribed speech retains speaker and demographic information that large language models can exploit for verification, though releasing only brief text excerpts reduces linkage risk.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Speech anonymization has focused on the acoustic signal, leaving open how much speaker information the transcribed words themselves retain. In this work, we study pairwise speaker and demographic-attribute verification from transcribed speech. We introduce SPARC (Spoken Profile Authorship Recognition Corpus), a benchmark built from two podcast corpora comprising over 12,500 speakers, 1.5M quality-filtered utterances, and speaker-level inferred or source-provided labels for sex, age, and accent, and evaluate five attribution models and three LLMs on identity- and attribute-verification trials with episode- and metadata-based topic proxies. LLM adversaries distinguish same-speaker pairs from text alone. Attribute-label agreement is harder to verify, but LLMs perform sex- and accent-label verification above chance. Finally, releasing only short probe texts (50 words) degrades the strongest adversaries the most, offering a partial reduction in observed linkage.

Abstract Harness-Delta Attribution dissects harness evolution gains into overfitting, test-time scaling, and residual generalizable improvement, finding that most search-set gains do not reliably transfer to held-out tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Harness evolution automatically optimizes the prompts, skills, tools, and orchestration code around a fixed-weight model, offering a way to improve agentic systems without updating model weights. The agent harness is iteratively optimized to achieve higher performance on the search set. But how much of this gain reflects benchmark-specific overfitting or additional inference-time compute, and how much may generalize beyond the search set? In this work, we introduce Harness-Delta Attribution (HDA), an evaluation protocol that dissects an evolution gain into artifact-driven overfitting, gains explained by test-time scaling, and the remainder, which gives an upper bound on residual generalizable improvement. We apply HDA to 11 executor models and three harness-evolution frameworks across math, coding, creativity, and agentic tasks. In 21 of these settings that show statistically significant search-set gains, we find that a large share of the improvement is explained by overfitting or test-time scaling. Specifically, among these 21 settings, overfitting and test-time scaling account for 54% and 14% of the gain on average; their combined share exceeds 50% in 14 settings. The residual and held-out transfer vary substantially across tasks, models, and frameworks. Importantly, search-set gains often fail to transfer to held-out tasks, and provide limited evidence that they will persist under practical distribution shifts. Moreover, validation-based signals are insufficient to prevent overfitting. These results motivate attribution-aware evaluation in future harness evolution research.

Abstract The study develops a taxonomy to identify legal guidance requests in conversational AI, annotates thousands of chats, and evaluates ModernBERT models against a baseline for detecting such queries.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small People ask conversational AI models about their legal rights, obligations, procedures, and personal situations that may have legal consequences. To study this behavior, we first need to distinguish requests for legal guidance from other legally themed discussions and unrelated conversations. We contribute a multi-axis taxonomy of legal queries capturing whether legal guidance is sought, how a request is formulated, and which legal domains it concerns; apply it to 2,122 naturally occurring WildChat-1M conversations; and create a human-reviewed gold evaluation set of 200 conversations. All annotations, model checkpoints, and evaluation code are publicly available. In this enriched sample, 71.1% of conversations containing legal content seek personalized or practical guidance. Rights-and-options advice is the most common primary request type among legal-content conversations, and 25.3% of guidance-positive conversations span multiple legal topics. The gold evaluation set supports a two-stage comparison of ModernBERT-base and ModernBERT-large against a TF-IDF cascade. Our framework supports large-scale studies of legal guidance in conversational AI, and the public release of the annotated data facilitates reproducible research and future model development.

Abstract Researchers benchmark vision-language models on simulated physical work tasks, finding strengths in text-based documentation but weaknesses in measurement reading, spatial reasoning, and acceptability judgment.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Augmenting worker capabilities in the physical world through guidance has been a decades-long goal for AI agents. One key supporting capability is to understand what the worker is doing, seeing through the human's camera or smart glasses, and assist them in documentation. Despite advances in vision language models, their capabilities in these settings remain uncertain and are challenging to benchmark because most physical work data is proprietary, and involves too much variability to study in a principled manner. Unlike structured settings such as kitchens and warehouses that have predictable lighting, geometry, and objects, field-facing tasks such as rooftop solar installation and vehicle repair are unconstrained and high variance. Here, we model real-world physical work in Blender across eleven environments, incorporating variants in both image acceptability and documentation. The acceptability task asks whether a particular view would be suitable proof-of-work and documentation asks whether an agent can understand the task-relevant state of the environment from that view. Across 26 vision-language models, we find that top models largely solve documentation when the answer is text in frame, but are weak at reading measurements and at judging small parts and spatial relations, such as whether a tape measure leans. Acceptability is harder to pin down because human raters disagree with each other. Measured against a scorer that agrees with the raters' majority about as often as a single rater does, the best models cannot be distinguished from a single non-author rater, at a cost within the range of a human pass. In the three environments where we can trace acceptability errors, most wrong rejections come from misreading the scene rather than the requirement. On the three documentation questions for which we have real images, model rankings on rendered frames transfer to real images.

Abstract HumanoidPose recovers full-body humanoid robot joint configurations from video by combining human-pose features with robot kinematics and self-training on unlabeled videos.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Estimating a humanoid robot's internal state from video has a wide range of real-world applications but remains unexplored. During interactions with a robot, its onboard state information may be unavailable due to privacy, ownership, or communication constraints. Meanwhile, online and AI-generated robot videos provide rich sources of data but typically lack corresponding joint configurations, limiting their direct use in robot learning. To address these issues, we present HumanoidPose, a new framework that treats vision as proprioception to recover full-body joint configurations in world space from video alone. Specifically, we combine pretrained human-pose features with robot kinematics in a unified model that predicts each embodiment's joint configurations. We retarget human motions to sixteen different humanoid embodiments, enabling scalable learning entirely from synthetic data. To improve real-world generalization, we further leverage unlabeled online videos through self-training to develop a 2D robot-pose detector. In our evaluation, HumanoidPose improves joint-angle and joint-position accuracy over the evaluated baselines and reduces joint-angle error by 18-32% relative to the best-performing baseline on each of the three real humanoid platforms. We further show improved global localization and generalization to unseen embodiments. HumanoidPose enables visual understanding of other robots during interaction and extraction of robot motion from online and generated videos.

Abstract First Passage Attention replaces monotonic decay accumulation with path extrema to selectively retain older context, enabling perfect long-range retrieval with compressed caches.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Augmenting softmax attention with forget gates typically relies on summing decay contributions along the key--query path. Because such accumulation is monotonic, these mechanisms cannot selectively erase intermediate context while preserving older, structurally relevant tokens. We propose First Passage Attention (FPA), which shifts from path summation to path extrema by lowering a key's logit proportionally to the deepest dip of a learned scalar field below that key's level over the intervening span. FPA encompasses the Forgetting Transformer (FoX) as a special case, and when the field and levels follow a shared learned walk, the alive keys with unpenalised logits naturally form a stack. We show that a constant-dimension FPA head realises any subset of keys left alive by subsequent dips, whereas softmax heads with positional biases or token-level forget gates require a head dimension linear in the number of keys. In synthetic experiments, one-layer FPA heads converge to these target sets while comparable softmax and FoX baselines fail on every seed. Scaling to a 110M-parameter language model, FPA matches FoX in perplexity and uniquely achieves perfect passkey retrieval at 8times the training length, even with its key--value cache compressed to about 25%.

Abstract Competence-based evaluation reveals that large language models fail to consistently apply reasoning rules across varied problem presentations despite high accuracy on bounded tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have brought impressive performance across diverse tasks; however, shortcomings remain in logical reasoning, including inconsistencies under linguistic variation. To improve the reasoning capacity of LLMs, we must be able to accurately measure their reasoning capacity. Current benchmarks primarily report single accuracy numbers on selected reasoning tasks. Such a performance-based evaluation methodology says little about a model's generalization capacity in reasoning. To address this limitation, we argue for a different evaluation paradigm, called competence-based evaluation (CBE), which tests whether models reliably apply the same competence across a family of problems. A parameterized generator scales complexity while keeping the required competence fixed, and matched answer-preserving presentations test correctness beyond one formulation. We instantiate CBE on elementary ordering tasks, scaling input size while varying presentations of the same ordering rule. Across six GPT and Qwen models, performance is reasonably good over bounded complexity ranges, but even strong models fail the competence condition across the tested family. Chain-of-thought and GRPO extend strong performance without resolving all tested failures; supervised LoRA shows little held-out transfer. CBE provides diagnostic profiles and an evaluation protocol that can grow with future models.
